J.5
Memory Consolidation Policies

Memory consolidation periodically merges short-term memories into long-term storage and updates vector indices. The consolidation function
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{M}_{s}\rightarrow(\mathcal{M}_{l},\mathcal{M}_{v})
operates in three phases:

Phase 1: Selection.

Determine which short-term memories warrant long-term retention. Messages are selected if:

\text{keep}(m)=(|m|>50)\land(r_{m}\neq\text{TOOL})\land(\mathcal{I}(m)>0.3)

(66)

This filters out trivial messages (very short), tool outputs (already logged separately), and low-importance content.

Phase 2: Transformation.

Convert selected messages to long-term memory entries with type inference:

t(m)=\begin{cases}\text{FACT}&\text{if contains }[\text{``is'', ``are'', ``equals''}]\\
\text{TASK}&\text{if }r_{m}=\text{USER}\land\text{contains}[\text{``do'', ``create'', ``find''}]\\
\text{REFLECTION}&\text{if contains }[\text{``learned'', ``realized'', ``understood''}]\\
\text{OBSERVATION}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(67)

Phase 3: Embedding and Indexing.

Generate embeddings for semantic search:

\mathbf{v}_{e}=\text{embed}(c_{e})\quad\text{and add to }\mathcal{M}_{v}

(68)

Consolidation runs every
N_{\text{messages}}=50
messages added to short-term memory or when triggered manually. The process is asynchronous to avoid blocking agent execution.

J.6
Memory Configuration and Tuning

Complete memory configuration involves setting parameters for all three tiers:

Memory System Configuration Example

memory:
short_term:
max_tokens: 4096 # Maximum tokens in short-term
max_messages: 100 # Maximum message count
summarization_threshold: 0.85 # Trigger at 85% of max_tokens
summary_ratio: 0.3 # Target 30% of original length
keep_recent: 10 # Always keep 10 most recent

long_term:
backend: sqlite # json | sqlite
path: ./memory/longterm.db # Storage path
retention_policy: importance # all | importance | recency
min_importance: MEDIUM # Minimum to retain
max_entries: 100000 # Cap at 100K entries
consolidation_interval: 50 # Every 50 messages

vector_store:
backend: faiss # faiss | chromadb
embedding_model: all-MiniLM-L6-v2
embedding_dim: 384
similarity_threshold: 0.7 # Min similarity for retrieval
max_results: 10 # Top-k results
index_type: flat # flat | ivf (for FAISS)
persist_path: ./memory/vectors

scoring:
weights:
recency: 0.30
frequency: 0.20
importance: 0.30
similarity: 0.20
decay_halflife_days: 7 # Recency decay parameter

J.7
Memory Efficiency Analysis

We analyze the space and time complexity of memory operations:

Storage Complexity.

Short-term memory uses
O(T_{\max})
space for bounded message deque. Long-term memory with JSON backend requires
O(n)
space for
n
entries. SQLite backend adds
O(n\log n)
for B-tree indices but provides much better query performance. Vector memory requires
O(n\cdot d)
for
n
vectors of dimension
d
.

Retrieval Complexity.

Short-term retrieval is
O(1)
for recent messages. Long-term retrieval with SQLite and proper indices is
O(\log n+k)
where
k
is result count. Vector similarity search is
O(n)
for flat index,
O(\sqrt{n})
for IVF, and
O(\log n)
for HNSW.

Consolidation Overhead.

Periodic consolidation processes
N_{\text{messages}}=50
messages, requiring
O(N_{\text{messages}}\cdot d)
for embedding generation and
O(N_{\text{messages}}\cdot\log n)
for index insertion. With
N_{\text{messages}}=50
and embedding time 8ms, consolidation takes approximately 400ms, negligible compared to typical conversation timescales (minutes).

J.9
Memory Privacy and Security

For privacy-sensitive deployments, the memory system implements several security measures:

Encryption at Rest.

All persistent storage backends support AES-256 encryption using the
cryptography
library. Encryption keys are derived from user-provided passwords using PBKDF2 with 100K iterations.

Access Control.

Each memory entry can have associated access control lists (ACLs) specifying which agents or users can retrieve it. This prevents information leakage between different users in multi-tenant deployments.

Selective Deletion.

The system supports targeted deletion by session ID, time range, or importance level, supporting compliance with data retention policies (e.g., GDPR’s right to erasure).

Audit Logging.

All memory operations (insert, retrieve, update, delete) generate audit logs with timestamps and requesting agent IDs, providing transparency for compliance requirements.

These features make
EffGen
suitable for enterprise deployments with strict security and compliance requirements.

Appendix K
Built-In Tool Specifications

The
EffGen
framework includes five built-in tools implementing the
BaseTool
interface: Calculator, Python REPL, Code Executor, File Operations, and Web Search. Tool-calling has emerged as a key capability for language model agents
(
73
;
97
;
81
;
66
)
, enabling structured reasoning and external API integration. Each tool provides parameter validation, error handling, and resource management. Figure
12
illustrates the complete pipeline: registry lookup for tool discovery, schema retrieval with metadata, parameter validation (required fields, types, constraints), sandboxed execution with timeout monitoring, result formatting, and integration back to the agent. This section specifies the mathematical foundations for the calculator, security properties of code execution tools, and performance measurements for all tools.

:
Tool calling pipeline with schema validation and sandboxed execution. The agent query triggers tool registry lookup for discovery, followed by schema retrieval containing metadata (name, description, parameters, validation rules, timeout, cost). Parameters undergo validation checking required fields, type matching, and constraints. Invalid parameters return errors immediately. Valid parameters proceed to extraction with type checking, then sandboxed execution with timeout monitoring across five built-in tools (Calculator with AST-safe evaluation, Python REPL, Code Executor with Docker isolation, File Operations, Web Search). K.1
Tool Interface and Metadata System

All tools inherit from
BaseTool
and declare metadata using
ToolMetadata
. The metadata structure
\mathcal{T}_{\text{meta}}=(n,d,c,p,v,\kappa,\theta,\mu)
consists of:

•

n
: tool name (unique identifier)

•

d
: natural language description for LLM understanding

•

c\in\mathcal{C}
: category from predefined taxonomy

•

p=\{(p_{i},t_{i},r_{i},d_{i})\}
: parameter specifications with name
p_{i}
, type
t_{i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\text{types}}
, required flag
r_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, default value
d_{i}

•

v
: version string (semantic versioning)

•

\kappa
: estimated cost (time, API calls, or monetary)

•

\theta
: default timeout in seconds

•

\mu
: additional metadata (tags, examples, constraints)

The tool category taxonomy
\mathcal{C}
includes eight categories optimized for agent understanding:
Information_Retrieval
,
Code_Execution
,
File_Operations
,
Computation
,
Communication
,
Data_Processing
,
System
,
External_API
.

Parameter Validation.

Before execution, parameters are validated against specifications. The validation function
\nu:P\times S\rightarrow\{0,1\}
checks three conditions: required parameters must be present, provided parameter types must match specifications, and parameter values must satisfy declared constraints. Formally:

\nu(p,s)=\bigwedge_{i}\left(r_{i}\Rightarrow(p_{i}\in s)\right)\land\left(p_{i}\in s\Rightarrow\text{type}(s[p_{i}])=t_{i}\right)\land\text{constraints}(s[p_{i}])

(69)

where
s
is the provided parameter dictionary. Validation failures generate structured error messages indicating the specific violation: missing required parameters, type mismatches between provided and expected types, or constraint violations such as out-of-range values.

K.2
Calculator Tool: Safe Mathematical Evaluation

The calculator tool provides arithmetic and mathematical function evaluation using Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) parsing, avoiding dangerous
eval()
or
exec()
operations that could execute arbitrary code.

Mathematical Operations.

The calculator supports 30+ operations organized into categories. Table
41
presents the complete taxonomy of supported operations:

Table 41
:

The calculator provides a comprehensive set of mathematical operations while maintaining security through strict AST-based validation that prevents code execution vulnerabilities.

AST-Based Evaluation.

Given expression string
s
, evaluation proceeds as:

1:

\text{ast\_tree}\leftarrow\text{ast.parse}(s,\text{mode}=\text{'eval'})

2:

Validate: all nodes in
ast_tree
are in whitelist
W=\{\text{BinOp},\text{UnaryOp},\text{Call},\text{Constant},\ldots\}

3:

If validation fails, raise
SecurityError

4:

Compile AST to bytecode:
\text{code}\leftarrow\text{compile}(\text{ast\_tree},\text{``<string>''},\text{'eval'})

5:

Evaluate with restricted namespace:
\text{result}\leftarrow\text{eval}(\text{code},\mathcal{N}_{\text{safe}})

6:

return

result

The safe namespace
\mathcal{N}_{\text{safe}}
contains only mathematical functions and constants, explicitly excluding file operations, imports, and system access. Expressions containing forbidden nodes (e.g.,
Import
,
FunctionDef
,
Attribute
) are rejected before evaluation.

Security Guarantees.

The AST validation provides strong security guarantees:

Theorem K.1

(Calculator Safety)
.

Let
s
be an expression string and
W
the whitelist of safe AST node types. If all nodes in
\text{parse}(s)
are in
W
and the evaluation namespace contains only pure functions (no side effects), then evaluating
s
cannot:

(a)

Access or modify the file system

(b)

Execute system commands

(c)

Import modules

(d)

Modify global state

(e)

Cause infinite loops (assuming bounded computation)

Proof.

AST whitelist
W
excludes all nodes capable of I/O (
Import
,
Open
), attribute access (
Attribute
), and general function definition (
FunctionDef
). The restricted namespace contains only mathematical functions implemented in C without Python-level side effects. Therefore, evaluation cannot access mechanisms for file I/O, system calls, or state modification.
∎

Performance Characteristics.

Calculator evaluation complexity is
O(n)
where
n
is the expression length (number of AST nodes). Typical expressions with 10-50 operations evaluate in 0.1-0.5ms. Table
42
presents microbenchmarks.

Table 42
:

The AST-based evaluation provides both security and performance, with typical expressions evaluating in 18-124 microseconds. As referenced in the main text, this performance supports real-time mathematical computations during agent reasoning.

K.3
Python REPL Tool: Persistent Session Execution

The Python REPL provides a persistent Python interpreter with session state preservation across multiple invocations. This supports iterative computation where variables defined in earlier steps are accessible in later steps.

Session Management.

Each REPL instance maintains a session dictionary
\mathcal{S}=\{v_{1}:val_{1},\ldots,v_{k}:val_{k}\}
storing variable bindings. Execution of code
c
modifies
\mathcal{S}
:

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\text{exec}(c,\mathcal{N}_{\text{restricted}},\mathcal{S})

(70)

where
\mathcal{N}_{\text{restricted}}
is the restricted builtin namespace and
\mathcal{S}
serves as both input (locals) and output (updated with new bindings).

Restricted Builtins.

To prevent malicious code execution, dangerous builtins are removed. Let
\mathcal{B}_{\text{all}}
be the set of all Python builtins and
\mathcal{B}_{\text{danger}}=\{\texttt{eval},\texttt{exec},\texttt{compile},\texttt{\_\_import\_\_},\texttt{open},\texttt{input},\texttt{breakpoint}\}
be the dangerous subset. The restricted namespace uses:

\mathcal{N}_{\text{restricted}}=\mathcal{B}_{\text{all}}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{\text{danger}}

(71)

Additionally, import statements are filtered through an allowlist
\mathcal{I}_{\text{allowed}}=\{\texttt{math},\allowbreak\texttt{random},\allowbreak\texttt{datetime},\allowbreak\texttt{json},\allowbreak\texttt{re},\allowbreak\texttt{collections},\allowbreak\texttt{itertools},\allowbreak\texttt{functools},\allowbreak\texttt{operator},\allowbreak\texttt{statistics},\allowbreak\texttt{decimal},\allowbreak\texttt{fractions}\}
Import attempts for modules outside
\mathcal{I}_{\text{allowed}}
raise
ImportError
.

Expression vs Statement Handling.

Python code can be either expressions (evaluating to values) or statements (performing actions). The REPL distinguishes these:

1:

Attempt to compile as expression:
compile(code, ’<stdin>’, ’eval’)

2:

if
compilation succeeds
then

3:

Evaluate and return result

4:

else

5:

Compile as statement:
compile(code, ’<stdin>’, ’exec’)

6:

Execute with session namespace

7:

Capture output from
stdout

8:

end

if

This supports natural interaction where
x = 5
(statement) assigns a variable while
x * 2
(expression) returns 10.

Output Capture.

Standard output and error streams are redirected during execution:

(\text{output},\text{error})=\text{redirect}(\text{stdout},\text{stderr},\lambda:\text{exec}(c,\mathcal{N},\mathcal{S}))

(72)

using Python’s
contextlib.redirect_stdout
and
redirect_stderr
. This captures print statements and error messages for inclusion in the tool result.

Security Analysis.

While the REPL restricts dangerous operations through namespace filtering, several attack vectors remain. Determined adversaries could attempt to access restricted modules via
__builtins__
manipulation, though this is mitigated by explicitly removing
__builtins__
from the execution namespace. Infinite loops represent another potential resource exhaustion attack, addressed through timeout enforcement that terminates execution after the configured duration. Memory exhaustion attacks are prevented through resource limits enforced at the sandbox level, capping memory allocation per execution. For maximum security in production deployments, the REPL should operate within a Docker sandbox (see Section
N
) providing OS-level isolation that prevents any breakout from the Python interpreter.

K.4
Code Executor Tool: Multi-Language Execution

The code executor supports multiple programming languages with sandboxed execution.

Sandbox Integration.

All code execution occurs within a sandbox environment (local or Docker-based) with strict resource limits enforced to prevent abuse. CPU time limits range from 30 to 60 seconds depending on language, with compiled languages receiving longer timeouts to accommodate compilation overhead. Memory is capped at 512MB by default, though this limit is configurable based on deployment requirements. Disk I/O is restricted to a temporary directory created per execution and destroyed afterward, preventing unauthorized file access. Network access is disabled by default to prevent data exfiltration, though this can be configured for specific use cases. Process spawning is limited to a maximum of 10 child processes to prevent fork bomb attacks. The sandbox configuration
\sigma=(\theta,\mu,\delta,\nu,\pi)
specifies timeout
\theta
, memory limit
\mu
, disk quota
\delta
, network policy
\nu\in\{\text{ALLOW},\text{DENY}\}
, and max processes
\pi
.

Compilation Caching.

For compiled languages, compilation results are cached to avoid redundant compilation:

\text{cache\_key}=\text{hash}(c+\text{compiler\_version}+\text{flags})

(73)

If
cache_key
exists in cache, compiled binary is reused. Otherwise, compilation occurs and result is cached. This reduces execution time by 3-10
\times
for repeated compilations.

K.5
File Operations Tool: Secure File System Access

The file operations tool provides read, write, list, and search capabilities with path validation and access control.

Supported Operations.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\text{ops}}=\{\text{READ},\text{WRITE},\text{LIST},\text{SEARCH},\text{DELETE},\text{MOVE},\text{COPY}\}
be the set of file operations. Each operation
\omega\in\mathcal{F}_{\text{ops}}
has associated permission
\pi_{\omega}\in\{\text{READ},\text{WRITE},\text{EXECUTE}\}
required for execution.

Path Validation.

All file paths undergo security validation before access. The validation function
\nu_{\text{path}}:\text{String}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
checks:

\nu_{\text{path}}(p)=\text{is\_absolute}(p)\land\neg\text{contains\_traversal}(p)\land p\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{allowed}}

(74)

where path traversal detection checks for patterns like
../
,
./
,
~/
that could escape allowed directories, and
\mathcal{P}_{\text{allowed}}
is the configured allowed path set (e.g., project directory only).

File Content Limits.

To prevent memory exhaustion from processing large files, the file operations tool enforces strict size limits on all operations. Read operations are capped at 10MB per file by default, preventing memory overflow from reading large log files or datasets. Write operations similarly limit output to 10MB, though both limits are configurable for specialized applications. List operations return at most 1000 directory entries to prevent performance degradation when scanning directories with millions of files. Search operations cap results at 100 matching files, returning the first 100 matches by relevance. Operations exceeding these limits return partial results along with warning messages indicating truncation, allowing agents to request more specific queries or process files in chunks.

Search Functionality.

The search operation finds files matching patterns. Given base directory
d
, pattern
p
, and options
\text{opts}=\{\text{recursive},\text{case\_sensitive},\text{max\_depth}\}
, search returns:

\text{search}(d,p,\text{opts})=\{f\in\text{walk}(d,\text{depth}):\text{match}(f,p,\text{opts})\}

(75)

where
walk
traverses the directory tree up to
max_depth
and
match
checks filename against pattern using glob or regex matching.

K.6
Web Search Tool: Internet Information Retrieval

The web search tool queries search engines and returns formatted results. Let
\mathcal{E}=\{\text{Google},\text{Bing},\text{DuckDuckGo}\}
be supported search engines. For query
q
and engine
e\in\mathcal{E}
, search returns
\{(t_{1},u_{1},s_{1}),\ldots,(t_{k},u_{k},s_{k})\}
where
t_{i}
is title,
u_{i}
is URL, and
s_{i}
is snippet.

Result Formatting.

Search results are formatted for LLM consumption with controlled verbosity. The formatting function
\phi:\mathcal{R}\times V\rightarrow\text{String}
takes results
\mathcal{R}
and verbosity
V\in\{\text{MINIMAL},\text{STANDARD},\text{DETAILED}\}
:

\phi(\mathcal{R},V)=\begin{cases}\text{join}(\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k})&V=\text{MINIMAL}\\
\text{join}(\{t_{i}+\text{': '}+u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k})&V=\text{STANDARD}\\
\text{join}(\{t_{i}+\text{' $\backslash$n '}+s_{i}+\text{' $\backslash$n '}+u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k})&V=\text{DETAILED}\end{cases}

(76)

For SLMs with limited context,
STANDARD
verbosity provides good information density (50-80 tokens per result) without overwhelming the context window.

Rate Limiting and Caching.

To avoid API rate limits and reduce latency, the web search tool implements three protective mechanisms. Request throttling limits queries to 10 searches per minute per engine, preventing the agent from overwhelming search APIs during iterative research. Result caching stores responses for identical queries, with a 5-minute validity window that balances freshness with efficiency. When rate limit errors occur, the tool employs exponential backoff, waiting
2^{n}
seconds before retry attempt
n
, allowing the API to recover before resuming queries. The cache uses a composite key combining engine, query, verbosity, and time bucket:

\text{cache\_key}=\text{hash}(e+q+V+t_{\text{now}}//300)

(77)

where division by 300 seconds creates 5-minute time buckets, meaning queries within the same 5-minute window receive cached results while older queries trigger fresh searches.

K.8
Tool Error Handling and Reliability

All tools implement error handling with structured error responses. The error response format
\mathcal{E}_{r}=(c,m,d,\sigma,\tau)
includes:

•

c
: error code (e.g.,
TIMEOUT
,
INVALID_INPUT
,
EXECUTION_FAILED
)

•

m
: human-readable error message

•

d
: detailed error information (stack traces, logs)

•

\sigma\in\{\text{RETRY},\text{FATAL}\}
: retryability classification

•

\tau
: timestamp

The agent uses retryability classification to decide whether to retry failed tool calls.
RETRY
errors (timeouts, rate limits) trigger exponential backoff retry, while
FATAL
errors (invalid parameters, security violations) do not retry.

Tool Health Monitoring.

The framework tracks tool health metrics:

\text{health}(t)=\frac{\text{successes}(t)}{\text{total\_calls}(t)}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\text{avg\_latency}(t)}{\text{timeout}(t)}\right)

(78)

Tools with
\text{health}(t)<0.5
trigger alerts for investigation. This supports proactive detection of degraded tools before complete failure.

Appendix L
Protocol Implementation Details

This section provides technical specifications for the three agent communication protocols implemented in
EffGen
. The framework represents the first unified implementation supporting MCP (Model Context Protocol), A2A (Agent-to-Agent), and ACP (Agent Communication Protocol) within a single system, supporting interoperability across different agent ecosystems. Figure
13
illustrates the complete architecture: requests route through protocol selection to MCP (tool/resource discovery), A2A (task lifecycle), or ACP (enterprise features), then flow through a unified transport layer (STDIO, HTTP, WebSocket, SSE) with message validation before unified response generation.

Figure 13
:
Multi-protocol agent communication system supporting MCP, A2A, and ACP. Agent requests route through protocol selection to one of three implementations: MCP (JSON-RPC 2.0) for tool/resource discovery, A2A for task lifecycle management with artifacts, or ACP for enterprise features with manifests and capability tokens. Each protocol accesses protocol-specific features then routes through a unified transport layer supporting STDIO, HTTP/REST, WebSocket, and SSE. Messages undergo validation and processing before unified response generation. Average message sizes range from 847-1231 bytes with parse times under 0.35ms.

L.1
Protocol Overview and Motivation

Modern agent systems require standardized communication mechanisms to support interoperability. Three major protocols have emerged from industry leaders: MCP
(
51
)
from Anthropic for tool and resource sharing, A2A
(
3
)
from Google for task-based agent collaboration, and ACP
(
2
)
from IBM for enterprise agent communication. All three protocols build on JSON-RPC 2.0
(
34
)
for message formatting.
EffGen
implements all three protocols with the following design goals:

•

Protocol Fidelity
: Strict adherence to official specifications to guarantee compatibility with external systems

•

Unified Interface
: Common abstractions allowing agents to switch protocols without code changes

•

Low-Overhead Implementation
: Message serialization optimized for SLMs with limited context

•

Type Safety
: Strongly-typed message structures with runtime validation

•

Extensibility
: Support for protocol-specific features while maintaining common interfaces

Table
44
compares the three protocols across key dimensions. Each protocol targets different use cases: MCP emphasizes tool discovery and context sharing, A2A focuses on task lifecycle management with artifacts, and ACP provides enterprise-grade features including agent manifests and capability tokens.

L.2
MCP (Model Context Protocol) Implementation

MCP follows JSON-RPC 2.0 specification with extensions for AI-specific capabilities. The protocol supports tools, resources, prompts, and sampling coordination between models and servers.

L.2.1
Message Structure

MCP defines four message types based on JSON-RPC 2.0:

MCP Message Types

•

Request
: Method invocation expecting response (contains
id
)

•

Response
: Result or error for request (matches
id
)

•

Notification
: One-way message (no
id
, no response)

•

Error
: Structured error with JSON-RPC 2.0 error codes

All messages share a common envelope structure:

M_{\text{MCP}}=\{\text{jsonrpc}:\text{"2.0"},\text{id}:i,\text{method}/\text{result}/\text{error}:\cdot\}

(79)

where
i\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\cup\{\text{string}\}
is the request identifier. The protocol enforces strict validation:
\text{jsonrpc}=\text{"2.0"}
must be present in all messages.

Request Message Format

Request messages invoke server methods with optional parameters:

MCP Request Schema

⬇

{

"
jsonrpc
":

"2.0",

"
id
":

1,

"
method
":

"
tools
/
call
",

"
params
":

{

"
name
":

"
calculator
",

"
arguments
":

{"
expression
":

"2+2"}

}

}

Response Message Format

Response messages return results or errors:

MCP Response Schema (Success)

⬇

{

"
jsonrpc
":

"2.0",

"
id
":

1,

"
result
":

{"
value
":

4,

"
type
":

"
number
"}

}

⬇

":

"2.0",

":

":

"}

}

}

L.2.4
Capabilities and Initialization

MCP uses a capability negotiation system during initialization. Both client and server declare supported features:

Capability Declaration

⬇

{

"
tools
":

true
,

"
resources
":

true
,

"
prompts
":

false
,

"

":

false
,

"
experimental
":

{

"
streaming
":

true
,

"
batch_operations
":

false

}

}

The initialization handshake:

\displaystyle\text{Client}\xrightarrow{\text{initialize}(\text{version},C_{\text{client}},\text{info})}\text{Server}

(81)

\displaystyle\text{Server}\xrightarrow{\text{result}(C_{\text{server}},\text{info})}\text{Client}

where
C_{\text{client}}
and
C_{\text{server}}
are capability sets. The effective capabilities are
C_{\text{eff}}=C_{\text{client}}\cap C_{\text{server}}
.

L.3.2
Task Lifecycle Management

A2A tasks progress through six states with atomic transitions:

S_{\text{task}}=\{\text{CREATED},\text{PENDING},\text{RUNNING},\text{COMPLETED},\text{FAILED},\text{CANCELLED}\}

(83)

State transitions follow this finite state machine:

\displaystyle\text{CREATED}\rightarrow\text{PENDING}\rightarrow\text{RUNNING}\rightarrow\{\text{COMPLETED}\mid\text{FAILED}\}

(84)

\displaystyle\text{CREATED}/\text{PENDING}/\text{RUNNING}\rightarrow\text{CANCELLED}

A2A Task Structure

L.3.3
Artifact System

Artifacts represent task outputs with rich metadata. Each artifact includes:

•

Unique ID
: UUID for artifact tracking

•

Type Classification
: result, intermediate, log, metric, visualization

•

Content
: Arbitrary JSON-serializable data

•

MIME Type
: Content type for binary data

•

Timestamps
: Creation and modification times

•

Metadata
: Custom key-value pairs

Artifacts accumulate during task execution:

A_{t}=A_{t-1}\cup\{a_{\text{new}}\}\quad\text{where}\quad|A_{t}|\geq|A_{t-1}|

(85)

Here
a_{\text{new}}
represents a newly generated artifact at time step
t
(such as a file, code snippet, or intermediate result), which gets added to the growing set of artifacts from previous steps.

Context is preserved across task chains:

\text{Context}_{i+1}=\text{Context}_{i}\cup\text{Updates}_{i}\setminus\text{Removals}_{i}

(86)

\forall t_{1}<t_{2}:\quad p(t_{1})\leq p(t_{2})\quad\text{where}\quad p(t)\in[0,1]

Appendix M
GPU Management and Parallelism Strategies

Small language model deployment on GPUs requires careful memory management and parallelism orchestration to maximize hardware utilization while preventing out-of-memory errors. The
EffGen
GPU management system provides automatic device allocation, memory estimation, real-time monitoring, and three parallelism strategies (tensor, pipeline, data) for scaling models beyond single-GPU capacity. Figure
14
illustrates the complete decision flow: memory estimation based on model parameters, allocation strategy selection (Greedy, Balanced, Optimized, Priority), single-GPU vs multi-GPU routing, and parallelism strategy selection (Tensor for 2-8x memory, Pipeline for 4x with divisible layers, Data for throughput). This section presents the mathematical foundations, implementation algorithms, and empirical characterization of the GPU management system.

Model

\text{mem}(|M|,p,L,B)

\alpha(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{G})

Figure 14
:
GPU management and parallelism strategy selection. The system estimates memory requirements using model size, precision, sequence length, and batch size, then selects an allocation strategy (Greedy, Balanced, Optimized with NVLink, or Priority). If the model fits on a single GPU, it allocates the best device. Otherwise, it routes to one of three parallelism strategies based on memory factor: Tensor parallelism (horizontal weight splitting, 85-92% efficiency) for 2-8x memory, Pipeline parallelism (layer-wise stages, 73% efficiency) for 4x memory with divisible layers, or Data parallelism (batch splitting, linear throughput) when single-device memory suffices but throughput is needed. The system monitors utilization throughout execution.

M.1
GPU Resource Model

We model a GPU cluster as
\mathcal{G}=\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\}
where each device
g_{i}
has properties
(M_{i},U_{i},T_{i},P_{i},\tau_{i})
:

•

M_{i}
: total memory capacity in bytes

•

U_{i}
: current memory utilization in bytes

•

T_{i}
: current GPU utilization %
[0,100]

•

P_{i}
: power consumption in watts

•

\tau_{i}
: temperature in celsius

The available memory is
A_{i}=M_{i}-U_{i}
. For allocation requests, we must guarantee sufficient memory:
\text{required}(r)\leq A_{i}
for some device
g_{i}
or across multiple devices for multi-GPU strategies.

Memory Estimation Function.

Given model parameters
|M|
(count), precision
p\in\{32,16,8,4\}
bits, sequence length
L
, and batch size
B
, the memory requirement estimate is:

\text{mem}(|M|,p,L,B)=\underbrace{\frac{|M|\cdot p}{8}}_{\text{weights}}+\underbrace{2\cdot|M|\cdot L\cdot B\cdot\frac{16}{8}}_{\text{activations}}+\underbrace{|M|\cdot L\cdot\frac{16}{8}}_{\text{KV cache}}+\underbrace{\alpha\cdot|M|\cdot\frac{32}{8}}_{\text{gradients}}

(89)

where
\alpha=0
for inference (no gradients) and
\alpha=1
for training. The factor 2 in activations accounts for forward and backward passes during training. This formula provides conservative estimates that prevent OOM errors in 95% of cases based on empirical validation.

M.2
Allocation Strategies

The allocation function
\alpha:\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
maps resource requests
\mathcal{R}
to device assignments
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
according to one of four strategies.

Greedy Strategy.

Allocate to the first device with sufficient memory:

\alpha_{\text{GREEDY}}(r,\mathcal{G})=\arg\min_{g_{i}\in\mathcal{G}}\{i:A_{i}\geq\text{mem}(r)\}

(90)

This strategy is simple and fast (
O(n)
for
n
devices) but can lead to unbalanced utilization where early devices are overloaded while later devices remain idle.

Balanced Strategy.

Allocate to the device with lowest utilization:

\alpha_{\text{BALANCED}}(r,\mathcal{G})=\arg\min_{g_{i}\in\mathcal{G}}\{U_{i}:A_{i}\geq\text{mem}(r)\}

(91)

This achieves better load distribution, with empirical studies showing 23% reduction in peak device utilization compared to greedy allocation.

Optimized Strategy.

For multi-GPU allocations (tensor or pipeline parallelism), minimize inter-GPU communication by selecting contiguous devices:

\alpha_{\text{OPTIMIZE}}(r,\mathcal{G},k)=\arg\min_{\{g_{i},g_{i+1},\ldots,g_{i+k-1}\}}\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\text{dist}(g_{i+j},g_{i+j+1})\right\}

(92)

where
k
is the number of required devices and
\text{dist}(g_{i},g_{j})
is the communication cost between devices (0 for same node, 1 for same rack, higher for cross-rack). This strategy uses NVLink (300 GB/s bandwidth) when available by allocating adjacent GPUs on the same node.

Priority Strategy.

Allocate best-performing devices (highest memory, lowest temperature) to high-priority requests:

\alpha_{\text{PRIORITY}}(r,\mathcal{G})=\arg\max_{g_{i}\in\mathcal{G}}\left\{\frac{A_{i}}{\max_{j}A_{j}}-\lambda\cdot\frac{T_{i}}{T_{\max}}:A_{i}\geq\text{mem}(r)\right\}

(93)

where
\lambda=0.3
balances memory availability against thermal constraints. This prevents assigning critical tasks to hot or memory-constrained devices.

M.3
Parallelism Strategies

When a model exceeds single-GPU capacity,
EffGen
employs one of three parallelism strategies. Let
M
be a model with
|M|
parameters and
d
layers.

Tensor Parallelism.

Partition each layer horizontally across
k
devices. For layer
\ell
with weight matrix
W_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, split column-wise:

W_{\ell}=[W_{\ell}^{(1)}|W_{\ell}^{(2)}|\cdots|W_{\ell}^{(k)}]\quad\text{where }W_{\ell}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n/k}

(94)

For forward pass with input
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, each device
i
computes
y^{(i)}=xW_{\ell}^{(i)}
, then results are concatenated:
y=[y^{(1)}|\cdots|y^{(k)}]
. Communication overhead is one all-reduce per layer.

Memory reduction:
\frac{\text{mem}(|M|,p,L,B)}{k}
(nearly linear scaling). Communication cost:
O(L\cdot B\cdot n)
per layer for all-reduce operation. Efficiency depends on inter-GPU bandwidth; on NVLink-connected GPUs, tensor parallelism achieves 85-92% efficiency for
k\leq 8
.

Pipeline Parallelism.

Partition layers into
k
stages, assigning consecutive layers to different devices:

\text{Stage}_{i}=\{\ell_{(i-1)\cdot d/k+1},\ldots,\ell_{i\cdot d/k}\}\quad\text{for }i\in[k]

(95)

For batch
\mathcal{B}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{B}\}
, split into
m
micro-batches
\{\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{m}\}
where
|\mathcal{B}_{j}|=B/m
. Execution proceeds as:

1:

for

j=1
to
m

do

2:

Device 1 processes
\mathcal{B}_{j}
through Stage 1, sends output to Device 2

3:

Device 2 processes
\mathcal{B}_{j}
through Stage 2, sends output to Device 3

4:

…(pipeline fill)

5:

end

for

6:

…(pipeline drain)

Memory reduction: approximately
\frac{\text{mem}(|M|,p,L,B)}{k}
since each device holds only
\approx d/k
layers. Communication cost:
O(m\cdot d\cdot L\cdot B/m)=O(d\cdot L\cdot B)
total. Pipeline efficiency is:

\eta_{\text{pipeline}}=\frac{m}{m+k-1}

(96)

where
m
is micro-batch count and
k
is device count. For
m=8
micro-batches and
k=4
devices,
\eta=0.73
(73% efficiency).

Data Parallelism.

Replicate the entire model on
k
devices and split the batch:

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{1}\cup\mathcal{B}_{2}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{B}_{k}\quad\text{where }|\mathcal{B}_{i}|=B/k

(97)

Each device processes its partition independently. For inference, no synchronization is needed. For training, gradients are averaged across devices after each step.

Memory impact: each device holds full model, so no memory reduction. However, throughput increases linearly:
\text{throughput}=k\cdot\text{throughput}_{\text{single}}
. This strategy is preferred when the model fits on a single device but throughput is insufficient.

Strategy Selection.

The system automatically selects the appropriate strategy based on model size and available resources. Algorithm
12
presents the decision logic.

M.4
Real-Time GPU Monitoring

The
GPUMonitor
class provides real-time tracking of device metrics using NVIDIA System Management Interface (nvidia-smi). Monitoring occurs in a background thread with configurable polling interval (default 1 second).

Collected Metrics.

For each device
g_{i}
, the monitor tracks:

•

Memory utilization:
(U_{i},A_{i},M_{i})

•

Compute utilization:
T_{i}\in[0,100]\%

•

Temperature:
\tau_{i}
in celsius

•

Power consumption:
P_{i}
in watts

•

Clock speeds: (graphics, SM, memory) in MHz

•

PCIe bandwidth utilization

Alerting Thresholds.

The monitor triggers alerts when metrics exceed safety thresholds:

CRITICAL

\displaystyle:(U_{i}/M_{i}>0.95)\lor(\tau_{i}>85\text{°C})\lor(P_{i}>0.95\cdot P_{\max})

(98)

WARNING

\displaystyle:(U_{i}/M_{i}>0.85)\lor(\tau_{i}>75\text{°C})\lor(P_{i}>0.85\cdot P_{\max})

(99)

These thresholds follow NVIDIA documentation and industry best practices: 85°C is the thermal throttling temperature for most NVIDIA data center GPUs (A100, H100), at which point the GPU automatically reduces clock speeds to prevent damage. The 95% memory threshold leaves approximately 400MB-2GB free (depending on GPU) for CUDA runtime overhead and memory fragmentation. Warning thresholds at 85% of critical values provide 10-15% buffer for graceful response before hitting limits.

Critical alerts trigger automatic mitigation: reduce batch size, throttle inference rate, or migrate workloads to cooler devices.

Historical Tracking.

Metrics are stored in a circular buffer with 1-hour retention (3600 samples at 1s interval). This supports trend analysis and anomaly detection. For example, steadily increasing temperature suggests cooling system degradation requiring maintenance.

M.5
Integration with Model Loaders

The GPU management system integrates with model loaders (vLLM, Transformers). For vLLM, the allocator sets the
CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES
environment variable before engine initialization:

allocation = allocator.allocate(model_config, strategy=TENSOR_PARALLEL)
os.environ["CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES"] = ",".join(map(str, allocation.devices))
engine = LLM(model=model_name, tensor_parallel_size=len(allocation.devices))

For multi-GPU strategies, vLLM handles internal communication and synchronization. The allocator guarantees devices are properly assigned and sufficient memory is available before initialization.

Graceful Degradation.

If GPU allocation fails (insufficient memory, no available devices), the system falls back gracefully:

1:

Attempt requested configuration (e.g., FP16, batch=8)

2:

if
allocation fails
then

3:

Reduce batch size: try batch=4, then batch=1

4:

end

if

5:

if
still fails
then

6:

Switch to quantization: try INT8

7:

end

if

8:

if
still fails
then

9:

Switch to CPU execution with warning

10:

end

if

This guarantees the agent can always execute, even if with reduced performance, rather than failing completely.

Appendix N
Execution Tracking and Observability

Transparent execution visibility is critical for debugging agent behavior, optimizing performance, and building trust in agent systems. The
EffGen
execution tracker implements a event-based tracking system capturing 17 distinct event types across the full agent lifecycle. Figure
15
illustrates the complete architecture: events flow from agent execution into five categories (Task Lifecycle, Routing/Decomposition, Sub-Agent Execution, Tool Execution, Memory/Special), forming a hierarchical tree structure with temporal consistency, status propagation, and uniqueness invariants, then metrics computation extracts 12 performance indicators for logs, analysis, and visualization. This section specifies the tracking architecture, event taxonomy, performance analysis capabilities, and visualization exports.

Figure 15
:
Execution tracking and observability system with event taxonomy. Events from agent execution flow into five categories arranged in three branches: Task Lifecycle and Routing/Decomposition (left), Sub-Agent Execution (center), and Tool Execution and Memory/Special (right). The system captures 17 distinct event types forming a hierarchical tree structure
(N,E,r)
. Metrics computation extracts 12 performance indicators including wall time, compute time, tool time, and 3-8% framework overhead. Outputs support logs, performance analysis, and visualization for debugging.

N.1
Event-Based Tracking Architecture

The execution tracker maintains a hierarchical tree structure
\mathcal{T}=(N,E,r)
where
N
is the set of execution nodes,
E\subseteq N\times N
are parent-child relationships, and
r\in N
is the root node. Each node
v\in N
represents an execution event
e_{v}=(t,\tau_{s},\tau_{e},s,m,c)
consisting of:

•

t\in\mathcal{E}_{\text{types}}
: event type from taxonomy

•

\tau_{s}
: start timestamp

•

\tau_{e}
: end timestamp (null if event ongoing)

•

s\in\{\text{PENDING},\text{RUNNING},\text{COMPLETED},\text{FAILED}\}
: status

•

m
: event-specific metadata (parameters, results, errors)

•

c\subseteq N
: child events

Tree Invariants.

The execution tree satisfies three invariants:

(i)

Temporal Consistency
: For parent-child pair
(p,c)\in E
,
\tau_{s}^{p}\leq\tau_{s}^{c}
and
\tau_{e}^{c}\leq\tau_{e}^{p}
(children execute within parent timespan)

(ii)

Status Propagation
: If any child has status FAILED, parent status must be FAILED or RUNNING

(iii)

Uniqueness
: Each execution event has unique identifier preventing duplication

These invariants support correct performance analysis and critical path identification.

N.2
Event Type Taxonomy

The event taxonomy
\mathcal{E}_{\text{types}}
contains 17 event types organized into five categories. Table
50
provides the complete specification.

Table 50
:
Execution Event Taxonomy. The framework tracks 17 distinct event types organized into five categories covering the complete agent lifecycle from task initiation through result synthesis. Each event type has specific metadata fields capturing relevant execution details. These events form a hierarchical tree structure (as described in the preceding text) supporting performance analysis, bottleneck detection, and critical path identification. Events are referenced throughout Section
N
for performance metric computation.

Category

Event Type

Description

Key Metadata Fields

Task Lifecycle

TASK_START

Agent begins processing task

task_description, agent_id, complexity_score

TASK_COMPLETE

Agent completes task successfully

final_result, token_count, execution_time

TASK_FAILED

Agent fails to complete task

error_message, failure_reason, partial_results

Routing & Decomp

ROUTING_DECISION

Router determines execution strategy

complexity_score, strategy, reasoning, confidence

TASK_DECOMPOSITION

Task decomposed into subtasks

num_subtasks, subtask_descriptions, dependencies

Sub-agent created for subtask

specialization, tools, prompt_template

Sub-agent results combined

num_results, synthesis_strategy, final_output

SUB_AGENT_START

Sub-agent begins execution

agent_id, specialization, assigned_task

Sub-agent completes successfully

result, tokens_used, tools_called

error, attempted_retries, fallback_strategy

thought, planned_action, iteration_count

Event Relationships.

Certain events have natural parent-child relationships forming execution patterns:

•

TASK_START
\rightarrow
ROUTING_DECISION
\rightarrow
{TASK_DECOMPOSITION | REASONING_STEP}

•

TASK_DECOMPOSITION
\rightarrow
SUB_AGENT_SPAWN
∗
(one spawn per subtask)

•

SUB_AGENT_START
\rightarrow
REASONING_STEP
∗

\rightarrow
TOOL_CALL_START
∗

\rightarrow
SUB_AGENT_COMPLETE

•

REASONING_STEP
\rightarrow
TOOL_CALL_START
\rightarrow
TOOL_CALL_COMPLETE

These patterns support pattern-based anomaly detection: deviation from expected event sequences indicates execution errors or unexpected agent behavior.

N.3
Performance Metrics Computation

The tracker computes 12 performance metrics from the execution tree. Let
T
denote total execution time,
N_{a}
the number of agents (1 for single,
\geq 2
for multi-agent), and
N_{t}
the number of tool calls.

Timing Metrics.

Define:

\displaystyle T_{\text{total}}

\displaystyle=\tau_{e}^{\text{root}}-\tau_{s}^{\text{root}}\quad\text{(wall-clock time)}

(100)

\displaystyle T_{\text{compute}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e:t_{e}=\text{REASONING\_STEP}}(\tau_{e}^{e}-\tau_{s}^{e})\quad\text{(pure reasoning time)}

(101)

\displaystyle T_{\text{tools}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e:t_{e}=\text{TOOL\_CALL}}(\tau_{e}^{e}-\tau_{s}^{e})\quad\text{(tool execution time)}

(102)

\displaystyle T_{\text{overhead}}

\displaystyle=T_{\text{total}}-T_{\text{compute}}-T_{\text{tools}}\quad\text{(framework overhead)}

(103)

The overhead includes routing decisions, task decomposition, result synthesis, and inter-agent communication. Typical overhead is 3-8% of total time.

Throughput Metrics.

Define:

\displaystyle\text{throughput}_{\text{tasks}}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{\text{completed\_subtasks}}}{T_{\text{total}}}\quad\text{(subtasks per second)}

(104)

\displaystyle\text{throughput}_{\text{events}}

\displaystyle=\frac{|N|}{T_{\text{total}}}\quad\text{(events per second)}

(105)

\displaystyle\text{throughput}_{\text{tools}}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{t}}{T_{\text{total}}}\quad\text{(tool calls per second)}

(106)

High-throughput agents achieve 5-10 subtasks/s with hundreds of events/s during parallel execution phases.

Resource Metrics.

Define:

peak_concurrent_agents

\displaystyle=\max_{t}|\{e\in N:t_{e}\in\{\text{SUB\_AGENT\_START}\}\land\tau_{s}^{e}\leq t<\tau_{e}^{e}\}|

(107)

total_tool_calls

\displaystyle=|\{e\in N:t_{e}=\text{TOOL\_CALL\_START}\}|

(108)

total_tokens

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in N}m_{e}[\text{tokens}]\quad\text{where defined}

(109)

These metrics inform resource provisioning decisions: high peak concurrency requires more GPU memory, many tool calls benefit from tool result caching.

Efficiency Metrics.

For multi-agent execution with parallel subtasks:

\text{parallel\_efficiency}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{a}}T_{i}}{N_{a}\cdot T_{\text{total}}}

(110)

where
T_{i}
is execution time of agent
i
. Perfect efficiency (
\eta=1.0
) means all agents busy entire time. Real systems achieve
\eta\in[0.6,0.9]
with inefficiency from load imbalance and coordination overhead.

Quality Metrics.

Define:

success_rate

\displaystyle=\frac{|\{e\in N:s_{e}=\text{COMPLETED}\}|}{|N|}

(111)

failure_rate

\displaystyle=\frac{|\{e\in N:s_{e}=\text{FAILED}\}|}{|N|}

(112)

retry_rate

\displaystyle=\frac{|\{e\in N:m_{e}[\text{retry\_count}]>0\}|}{|N|}

(113)

Healthy agents maintain success_rate
>0.95
, failure_rate
<0.05
, and retry_rate
<0.1
.

N.4
Bottleneck Identification

The tracker identifies performance bottlenecks through automated analysis. Algorithm
13
presents the detection procedure.

Algorithm 13

Bottleneck Detection in Execution Tree

1:

Execution tree
\mathcal{T}=(N,E,r)

2:

List of bottlenecks with severity

3:

\text{bottlenecks}\leftarrow[]

4:

T_{\text{total}}\leftarrow\tau_{e}^{r}-\tau_{s}^{r}

5:

for
each node
v\in N

do

6:

T_{v}\leftarrow\tau_{e}^{v}-\tau_{s}^{v}

7:

\text{contribution}_{v}\leftarrow T_{v}/T_{\text{total}}

8:

if

\text{contribution}_{v}>0.3

then

9:

Add
(v,\text{HIGH},\text{contribution}_{v})
to bottlenecks
\triangleright
30% threshold

10:

else

if

\text{contribution}_{v}>0.15

then

11:

Add
(v,\text{MEDIUM},\text{contribution}_{v})
to bottlenecks

12:

end

if

13:

end

for
\triangleright
Check for slow tools

14:

for
each tool call event
e
with type TOOL_CALL_COMPLETE
do

15:

\text{avg\_time}\leftarrow\text{average\_tool\_time}(e.\text{tool\_name})

16:

if

(\tau_{e}^{e}-\tau_{s}^{e})>2\cdot\text{avg\_time}

then

17:

Add
(e,\text{SLOW\_TOOL},\text{slowdown\_factor})
to bottlenecks
\triangleright
2x slower

18:

end

if

19:

end

for
\triangleright
Check for high failure rates

20:

for
each sub-agent
a

do

21:

\text{failures}_{a}\leftarrow|\{e\in\text{children}(a):s_{e}=\text{FAILED}\}|

22:

if

\text{failures}_{a}/|\text{children}(a)|>0.2

then

23:

Add
(a,\text{HIGH\_FAILURES},\text{failure\_rate})
to bottlenecks
\triangleright
20% failure

24:

end

if

25:

end

for

26:

return
sorted(bottlenecks, key=severity, reverse=True)

Bottleneck detection identifies: (1) individual events consuming
>
30% of total time, (2) tools performing
>
2
\times
slower than average, and (3) agents with
>
20% failure rate. This information guides optimization: slow tools can be replaced or optimized, high-failure agents need better prompts or different model sizes.

N.5
Critical Path Analysis

For parallel execution, the critical path determines the minimum possible execution time. The critical path
\pi_{\text{crit}}=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})
is the longest path from root to leaf in the execution tree:

\pi_{\text{crit}}=\arg\max_{\pi:r\rightarrow\text{leaf}}\sum_{v\in\pi}(\tau_{e}^{v}-\tau_{s}^{v})

(114)

The critical path length
L_{\text{crit}}=\sum_{v\in\pi_{\text{crit}}}(\tau_{e}^{v}-\tau_{s}^{v})
represents the theoretical minimum execution time even with infinite parallelism. The parallel efficiency is bounded by:

\eta\leq\frac{L_{\text{crit}}}{T_{\text{total}}}

(115)

Events on the critical path are prioritized for optimization: reducing their duration directly reduces total execution time, while optimizing off-critical-path events has no effect on wall-clock time (though it may reduce resource usage).

N.7
Live Monitoring and Progress Tracking

For long-running tasks, the tracker provides live progress updates. The progress estimation function combines completed and in-progress work:

\text{progress}(t)=\frac{\sum_{e:s_{e}=\text{COMPLETED}}w_{e}+\sum_{e:s_{e}=\text{RUNNING}}0.5\cdot w_{e}}{\sum_{e\in N}w_{e}}

(116)

where
w_{e}
is the estimated work for event
e
(for subtasks,
w_{e}=1
; for tool calls,
w_{e}=0.1
). The factor 0.5 for running events assumes half-completion on average.

ETA Estimation.

Estimated time to completion uses velocity-based projection:

\text{ETA}(t)=t+\frac{1-\text{progress}(t)}{\text{velocity}(t)}\quad\text{where }\text{velocity}(t)=\frac{\text{progress}(t)}{t-t_{\text{start}}}

(117)

For tasks with
<
10% progress, ETA is unreliable and marked as unknown. After 10% completion, typical ETA accuracy is
\pm
30%.

N.8
Integration with External Monitoring

The tracker integrates with external monitoring systems through two mechanisms:

OpenTelemetry Export.

Events are exported as OpenTelemetry spans with parent-child relationships preserved. This supports integration with distributed tracing systems (Jaeger, Zipkin) for multi-service observability.

Prometheus Metrics.

Key metrics are exposed as Prometheus gauges and counters:

Prometheus Metrics Export

⬇

effgen_task_duration_seconds
{
status
="
completed
"}

effgen_tool_calls_total
{
tool_name
="
calculator
"}

effgen_agent_errors_total
{
error_type
="
timeout
"}

...

This supports alerting based on metric thresholds and long-term performance trending.

Appendix O
Configuration System Architecture

The
EffGen
configuration system provides a configurable, validated, and extensible approach to agent setup through YAML and JSON formats. The system supports environment variable substitution, configuration merging, schema validation, and hot reloading for both development and production deployments. This section presents the complete configuration architecture with mathematical specifications, validation algorithms, and empirical performance analysis.

O.1
Configuration File Format

EffGen
configurations follow a hierarchical structure with four top-level sections:
agent
,
model
,
tools
, and
memory
. The configuration space
\mathcal{C}
is defined as the cross product:

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\text{agent}}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{model}}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{tools}}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{memory}}

(118)

where each component space has specific schema constraints enforced through JSON Schema validation.

Complete Configuration Schema.

The full specification with all optional parameters:

Complete Configuration Schema (
agent_config.yaml
)

# Agent metadata and high-level configuration
agent:
name: string (required)
description: string (optional)
version: string (optional, semantic version)
tags: list[string] (optional, for organization)

# Model configuration section
model:
name: string (required, HuggingFace model ID or API model)
backend: string (optional, auto-detect if not specified)
# Options: vllm, transformers, openai, anthropic, gemini
generation_config:
max_tokens: int (default: 4096, range: [1, 32768])
temperature: float (default: 0.0, range: [0.0, 2.0])
top_p: float (default: 0.9, range: [0.0, 1.0])
top_k: int (optional, range: [1, 100])
frequency_penalty: float (default: 0.0, range: [-2.0, 2.0])
presence_penalty: float (default: 0.0, range: [-2.0, 2.0])
repetition_penalty: float (default: 1.0, range: [1.0, 2.0])
stop_sequences: list[string] (optional)
quantization: string (optional, null/int8/int4)
device: string (optional, auto/cuda/cpu)
device_map: string (optional, auto/balanced/sequential)
offload_folder: string (optional, path for offloading)
gpu_memory_utilization: float (default: 0.9, range: [0.1, 1.0])
tensor_parallel_size: int (optional, for multi-GPU)
api_key: string (optional, for API-based models)
api_base: string (optional, custom API endpoint)

# Complexity analysis and routing configuration
complexity:
enabled: bool (default: true)
threshold: float (default: 7.0, range: [0.0, 10.0])
hierarchical_threshold: float (default: 8.5, range: [0.0, 10.0])
weights:
task_length: float (default: 0.15, constraint: sum=1.0)
requirement_count: float (default: 0.25)
domain_breadth: float (default: 0.20)
tool_requirements: float (default: 0.20)
reasoning_depth: float (default: 0.20)
custom_domains: dict[string, list[string]] (optional)
custom_tool_categories: dict[string, list[string]] (optional)

# Prompt optimization configuration
prompt_optimizer:
enabled: bool (default: true)
model_size: string (required if enabled)
# Options: tiny (<1B), small (1-3B), medium (3-7B), large (7B+)
max_prompt_tokens: int (default: auto based on model_size)
max_context_tokens: int (default: auto based on model_size)
compression_ratio: float (default: auto, range: [0.2, 1.0])
enable_compression: bool (default: true)
enable_simplification: bool (default: true)
enable_redundancy_removal: bool (default: true)
enable_bullet_formatting: bool (default: true)
enable_truncation: bool (default: true)
custom_patterns: list[dict] (optional, {pattern: str, replacement: str})

# Tools configuration
tools:
- name: string (required, builtin tool name or custom)
enabled: bool (default: true)
config: dict (tool-specific configuration)
timeout: int (default: 60, seconds)
max_retries: int (default: 3)
retry_delay: float (default: 2.0, seconds)
validate_inputs: bool (default: true)
validate_outputs: bool (default: true)
description: string (optional, override default)
priority: int (default: 0, higher=higher priority)

# Memory system configuration
memory:
enabled: bool (default: true)

short_term:
enabled: bool (default: true)
max_messages: int (default: 100, range: [10, 1000])
max_tokens: int (default: 4096, range: [512, 32768])
summarization_threshold: float (default: 0.85, range: [0.5, 1.0])
summary_strategy: string (default: recent)
# Options: recent, important, mixed
preserve_recent_count: int (default: 10)

long_term:
enabled: bool (default: true)
backend: string (default: sqlite)
# Options: json, sqlite
path: string (default: ./memories/agent.db)
max_entries: int (optional, null for unlimited)
retention_days: int (optional, null for unlimited)
importance_threshold: float (default: 0.3, range: [0.0, 1.0])
consolidation_interval: int (default: 50, messages)

vector_memory:
enabled: bool (default: false)
backend: string (required if enabled)
# Options: faiss, chroma
embedding_model: string (default: all-MiniLM-L6-v2)
embedding_dimension: int (auto from model)
similarity_threshold: float (default: 0.7, range: [0.0, 1.0])
max_results: int (default: 5, range: [1, 20])
index_type: string (default: auto)
# Options: auto, flat, ivf, hnsw
index_path: string (default: ./memories/vector_index)

# Sub-agent configuration
sub_agents:
enabled: bool (default: true)
max_depth: int (default: 2, range: [1, 5])
max_concurrent: int (default: 5, range: [1, 20])
spawn_strategy: string (default: auto)
# Options: auto, never, always
inherit_tools: bool (default: true)
inherit_memory: bool (default: false)
communication_protocol: string (default: internal)
# Options: internal, mcp, a2a, acp

# Execution tracking and logging
execution_tracking:
enabled: bool (default: true)
log_level: string (default: INFO)
# Options: DEBUG, INFO, WARNING, ERROR
log_file: string (optional, path to log file)
track_tokens: bool (default: true)
track_latency: bool (default: true)
track_tool_calls: bool (default: true)
track_memory_operations: bool (default: false)
export_traces: bool (default: false)
trace_format: string (default: json)
# Options: json, opentelemetry

# Execution control parameters
execution:
max_iterations: int (default: 10, range: [1, 100])
early_stopping: bool (default: true)
timeout: int (default: 300, seconds)
parallel_tool_calls: bool (default: false)
tool_call_timeout: int (default: 60, seconds)
retry_on_error: bool (default: true)
max_retries: int (default: 3)
backoff_factor: float (default: 2.0, exponential backoff)

# Protocol configurations (for external communication)
protocols:
mcp:
enabled: bool (default: false)
transport: string (default: stdio)
# Options: stdio, http, websocket, sse
host: string (default: localhost)
port: int (default: 3000)

a2a:
enabled: bool (default: false)
endpoint: string (required if enabled)
agent_card_path: string (optional)

acp:
enabled: bool (default: false)
manifest_path: string (required if enabled)
capability_tokens: list[string] (optional)

O.2
Environment Variable Substitution

The configuration system supports environment variable substitution using the syntax
${VAR_NAME}
or
${VAR_NAME:default_value}
. The substitution function
\sigma:\text{String}\rightarrow\text{String}
is defined as:

\sigma(s)=s[\text{\$\lx@text@lbrace v\lx@text@rbrace}\mapsto\text{getenv}(v,d)\quad\forall v\in\text{vars}(s)]

(119)

where
\text{vars}(s)
extracts all variable references and
\text{getenv}(v,d)
retrieves environment variable
v
or default
d
.

Environment Variable Examples.

Configuration supports dynamic values through environment variable substitution:

Environment Variable Substitution Examples

model:
name: ${MODEL_NAME:Qwen/Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct}
api_key: ${OPENAI_API_KEY} # Required if using API models
api_base: ${API_BASE:https://api.openai.com/v1}

memory:
long_term:
path: ${MEMORY_PATH:./memories/agent.db}
vector_memory:
index_path: ${VECTOR_INDEX_PATH:./memories/vector}

execution_tracking:
log_file: ${LOG_FILE:/var/log/effgen/agent.log}

Usage with environment:

export MODEL_NAME="Qwen/Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct"
export OPENAI_API_KEY="sk-..."
python run_agent.py --config agent_config.yaml

O.3
Schema Validation

All configurations are validated against JSON Schema definitions before agent initialization. The validation function
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\{\text{valid},\text{invalid}\}
checks:

1.

Type Constraints
: All fields have correct types (string, int, float, bool, list, dict)

2.

Range Constraints
: Numeric values within specified ranges (e.g.,
\text{temperature}\in[0,2]
)

3.

Enum Constraints
: String values from allowed sets (e.g., backend
\in\{\text{vllm},\text{transformers},\ldots\}
)

4.

Dependency Constraints
: Required fields present when dependent features enabled

5.

Sum Constraints
: Weight vectors sum to 1.0 (complexity factor weights)

6.

Mutual Exclusivity
: Conflicting options not simultaneously enabled

Validation Algorithm.

Algorithm
14
presents the complete validation procedure.

Algorithm 14

Configuration Validation

1:

Configuration dict
c
, Schema definition
\mathcal{S}

2:

Validated configuration or error list

3:

\text{errors}\leftarrow[\,]

4:

\text{warnings}\leftarrow[\,]

5:

6:

\triangleright
Phase 1: Type and structure validation

7:

if
NOT
validateStructure
(c,
\mathcal{S}
)
then

8:

Append structural errors to
errors

9:

end

if

10:

11:

\triangleright
Phase 2: Range constraints

12:

for
each numeric field
f
in
c

do

13:

if

f
has range constraint
[l,u]
in
\mathcal{S}

then

14:

if

c[f]<l

Append “Field {f} out of range” to
errors

16:

end

17:

end

end

for

\triangleright

for
each enum field
f
in
c

O.4
Configuration Merging and Inheritance

EffGen
supports configuration inheritance through the
extends
keyword, supporting modular configuration management. Given base configuration
c_{b}
and override configuration
c_{o}
, the merge function
\mu:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
produces merged configuration:

\mu(c_{b},c_{o})=\begin{cases}c_{o}[k]&\text{if }k\in c_{o}\land\text{typeof}(c_{o}[k])\neq\text{dict}\\
\mu(c_{b}[k],c_{o}[k])&\text{if }k\in c_{o}\land\text{typeof}(c_{o}[k])=\text{dict}\\
c_{b}[k]&\text{if }k\notin c_{o}\end{cases}

(120)

This performs deep merging: nested dictionaries merge recursively, while scalar values and lists are overridden.

Configuration Inheritance Example.

This section shows an example of base and production configuration.

Base Configuration (
base_config.yaml
)

agent:
name: base_agent

model:
backend: vllm
generation_config:
temperature: 0.0
top_p: 0.9
max_tokens: 4096

tools:
- name: calculator
- name: web_search

memory:
short_term:
max_tokens: 4096

Production Configuration (
prod_config.yaml
)

extends: base_config.yaml

agent:
name: production_agent # Override name
version: "1.0.0" # Add version

model:
name: Qwen/Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct # Specify model
generation_config:
temperature: 0.7 # Override temperature, inherit other params

tools: # Completely replace tools list
- name: web_search
- name: python_repl
- name: file_operations

memory:
long_term: # Add long-term memory, inherit short-term
enabled: true
backend: sqlite
path: /var/lib/effgen/memory.db

The resulting merged configuration combines base defaults with production overrides, supporting DRY (Don’t Repeat Yourself) configuration management.

O.5
Hot Reloading

For long-running agents,
EffGen
supports hot reloading of configuration without restart. The reload function
\rho:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
updates agent
\mathcal{A}
with new configuration
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, preserving state when possible:

Algorithm 15

Hot Configuration Reload

1:

Agent
\mathcal{A}
, New configuration
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

2:

Updated agent
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

3:

Validate
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
using
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})

4:

if
validation fails
then

5:

return
error

6:

end

if

7:

8:

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}.\text{config}\leftarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

9:

10:

\triangleright
Reload model if changed

11:

if

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.\text{model}.\text{name}\neq\mathcal{A}.\text{model}.\text{name}

then

12:

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}.\text{model}\leftarrow\text{LoadModel}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.\text{model})

13:

else

14:

Update model generation config

15:

end

if

16:

17:

\triangleright
Reload tools if changed

18:

if

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.\text{tools}\neq\mathcal{A}.\text{config}.\text{tools}

then

19:

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}.\text{tools}\leftarrow\text{LoadTools}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.\text{tools})

20:

end

if

21:

22:

\triangleright
Update complexity thresholds

23:

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}.\text{analyzer}.\text{threshold}\leftarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.\text{complexity}.\text{threshold}

24:

25:

\triangleright
Preserve memory and state

26:

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}.\text{memory}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}.\text{memory}

\triangleright
Keep existing memories

27:

28:

return

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

Hot Reload Usage.

Configuration changes can be applied to running agents without restart:

Hot Reload Example

# Initial agent creation
agent = Agent.from_config("agent_config.yaml")

# Agent runs for some time...
response1 = agent.run("Query 1")
response2 = agent.run("Query 2")

# Update configuration file externally
# (e.g., change temperature, add tools, adjust thresholds)

# Hot reload without restart
agent.reload_config("agent_config.yaml")

# Agent continues with new configuration
response3 = agent.run("Query 3") # Uses new config

Hot reloading preserves critical agent state to maintain continuity across configuration updates. The memory system retains both short-term and long-term memories, ensuring conversation context survives the reload. Session state and conversation history remain intact, allowing multi-turn interactions to continue without interruption. Execution statistics accumulated prior to reload are preserved for analytics and monitoring. The sub-agent registry maintains references to spawned agents, preventing orphaned processes.

Reloaded components include model weights (if the model name changed), requiring re-loading from disk or API. The tool set is refreshed when the configuration tool list differs from the current set. Generation parameters (temperature, top-p, max tokens) update immediately without model reload. Complexity thresholds for routing decisions apply to subsequent queries. Prompt optimizer settings take effect on the next prompt construction, applying new compression or formatting rules.

Appendix P
Command-Line Interface

The
EffGen
command-line interface provides simplified access to framework functionality for development, testing, and production deployment. The CLI organizes commands into logical groups with consistent syntax, help text, and support for both interactive and batch operations. This section documents all CLI commands with usage examples, configuration options, and integration patterns. For the latest CLI documentation and interactive tutorials, visit
https://docs.effgen.org/cli
.

P.1
CLI Command Structure

The
EffGen
CLI follows the pattern
effgen <command> [subcommand] [options] [arguments]
. Commands are organized hierarchically:

P.2
Core Commands

P.2.1
Run Command

Execute agent on single query with immediate result.

effgen run
- Single Query Execution

Usage: effgen run [OPTIONS] QUERY

Options:
-c, --config PATH Configuration file path [default: ./agent_config.yaml]
-m, --model TEXT Model name (overrides config)
-t, --tools TEXT Comma-separated tool names
--temperature FLOAT Sampling temperature [0.0-2.0]
--max-tokens INT Maximum tokens to generate
--timeout INT Execution timeout in seconds [default: 300]
--session-id TEXT Session ID for memory persistence
--stream Support streaming output
--verbose Show detailed execution trace
--output FORMAT Output format: text, json, markdown [default: text]
-o, --output-file PATH Save result to file
--help Show help and exit

Examples:
# Basic usage
effgen run "What is 2+2?"

# With custom configuration
effgen run -c prod_config.yaml "Research quantum computing trends"

# Override model and temperature
effgen run -m Qwen/Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct --temperature 0.7 "Write a poem"

# With specific tools
effgen run -t calculator,web_search "Calculate GDP growth for G7"

# JSON output to file
effgen run --output json -o result.json "Analyze this data"

# Streaming with verbose trace
effgen run --stream --verbose "Complex research task..."

P.2.2
Interactive Command

Start conversational session with multi-turn interactions.

effgen interactive
- Conversation Mode

Usage: effgen interactive [OPTIONS]

Options:
-c, --config PATH Configuration file path
--session-id TEXT Resume existing session
--save-history Save conversation history to file
--multiline Support multiline input mode
--help Show help and exit

Special Commands (in interactive mode):
/help Show available commands
/clear Clear conversation history
/save [file] Save current conversation
/load [file] Load previous conversation
/config [key] [value] Update configuration parameter
/stats Show execution statistics
/exit, /quit Exit interactive mode

Example Session:
$ effgen interactive -c agent_config.yaml
EffGen Interactive Session (Ctrl+D or /exit to quit)
> What is machine learning?
[Agent responds...]
> Can you explain supervised learning in detail?
[Agent responds, recalling context...]
> /stats
Session Statistics:
Messages: 4
Tokens used: 1,247
Tool calls: 0
> /exit

P.3
Configuration Commands

Configuration Management Commands

# Validate configuration file
effgen config validate [PATH]
--strict Support strict validation (warnings as errors)
--fix Auto-fix correctable issues

# Generate template configuration
effgen config generate [OPTIONS]
--model-size TEXT Model size: tiny, small, medium, large
--tools TEXT Comma-separated tool names
--support-memory Include memory configuration
--output PATH Output file path [default: stdout]

# Show effective configuration (after merging, env vars)
effgen config show [PATH]
--format FORMAT Output format: yaml, json [default: yaml]
--resolve-env Resolve environment variables

Examples:
effgen config validate prod_config.yaml
effgen config generate --model-size medium --tools calculator,web_search
effgen config show --resolve-env agent_config.yaml

Model Management Commands

# List available models
effgen model list [OPTIONS]
--backend TEXT Filter by backend: vllm, transformers, api
--size-range TEXT Size range: 1B-7B, 7B-13B, 13B+
--local-only Show only locally downloaded models

# Download model from HuggingFace
effgen model download MODEL_NAME [OPTIONS]
--quantization TEXT Quantization: int8, int4
--cache-dir PATH Download cache directory

# Show model information
effgen model info MODEL_NAME
--show-config Include generation config details

Examples:
effgen model list --backend vllm --size-range 1B-7B

P.5

Tool Management Commands

# List available tools
effgen tool list [OPTIONS]
--builtin Show only built-in tools
--custom Show only custom tools
--protocol TEXT Filter by protocol: mcp, a2a, acp

# Test tool execution
effgen tool test TOOL_NAME [OPTIONS]
--input JSON Tool input as JSON string
--input-file PATH Tool input from file
--timeout INT Execution timeout

# Register custom tool
effgen tool register [OPTIONS]
--name TEXT Tool name
--description TEXT Tool description
--script PATH Tool implementation script
--protocol TEXT Protocol: native, mcp, a2a, acp

Examples:
effgen tool list --builtin
effgen tool test calculator --input ’{"expression": "2+2"}’

P.6
Memory Commands

Memory Management Commands

# Export memories to file
effgen memory export [OPTIONS]
--session-id TEXT Export specific session
--format FORMAT Format: json, sqlite [default: json]
--output PATH Output file path

# Import memories from file
effgen memory import PATH [OPTIONS]
--merge Merge with existing memories
--overwrite Overwrite existing entries

# Clear memory
effgen memory clear [OPTIONS]
--session-id TEXT Clear specific session
--confirm Skip confirmation prompt

Examples:
effgen memory export --session-id user_123 --output backup.json
effgen memory import backup.json --merge
effgen memory clear --session-id test_session --confirm

P.7
Benchmark Commands

Benchmarking Commands

# Run benchmark
effgen benchmark run [OPTIONS]
--config PATH Agent configuration
--benchmark TEXT Benchmark name: gsm8k, arc, gaia, etc.
--samples INT Number of samples [default: all]
--output PATH Results output path
--parallel INT Parallel execution workers

# Generate benchmark report
effgen benchmark report [OPTIONS]
--results PATH Results file or directory
--format FORMAT Format: text, json, markdown, latex
--compare PATH Compare with baseline results

Examples:
effgen benchmark run --config agent.yaml --benchmark gsm8k
effgen benchmark report --results results/ --format latex
effgen benchmark report --results new.json --compare baseline.json

HTTP API Server Commands

# Start HTTP API server
effgen server start [OPTIONS]
--config PATH Agent configuration
--host TEXT Server host [default: 0.0.0.0]
--port INT Server port [default: 8000]
--workers INT Number of worker processes
--reload Support auto-reload on code changes

# Stop running server
effgen server stop

# Show server status
effgen server status

Examples:
effgen server start --config agent.yaml --port 8080
effgen server start --reload # Development mode

P.10
Batch Processing with Scripts

The CLI supports scripting through standard input and output redirection:

Batch Processing Examples

# Process queries from file
cat queries.txt | while read query; do
effgen run "$query" --output json >> results.jsonl
done

# Parallel batch processing
parallel -j 4 effgen run ::: query1 query2 query3 query4

# Generate reports for multiple benchmarks
for bench in gsm8k arc gaia; do
effgen benchmark run --benchmark $bench --output results_$bench.json
done
effgen benchmark report --results results_*.json --format latex

The CLI interface provides access to all
EffGen
functionality with consistent, user-friendly commands suitable for both interactive use and automated workflows.

Appendix Q
Model Loading and Backend Selection

The
EffGen
model loading system provides a unified interface across multiple inference backends with automatic selection, failover mechanisms, and performance optimization. This section presents the complete model abstraction architecture, backend comparison with empirical benchmarks, automatic selection logic, and quantization support for resource-constrained deployments.

Q.1
Model Abstraction Architecture

All models in
EffGen
conform to the abstract interface
\mathcal{I}_{M}
defining three core operations:

\mathcal{I}_{M}=\{\text{generate}:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{R},\text{batch\_generate}:\mathcal{P}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}^{n},\text{stream}:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\text{Iterator}[\mathcal{R}]\}

(121)

where
\mathcal{P}
is the prompt space and
\mathcal{R}
is the response space. This abstraction supports transparent backend switching without code modifications. The model loader
\mathcal{L}:\text{Config}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{M}
instantiates the appropriate backend based on model name, availability, and hardware.

Q.2
Supported Backends

EffGen
supports five inference backends, each optimized for different deployment scenarios. The vLLM backend
(
37
;
38
)
uses PagedAttention for efficient memory management. Quantization support includes INT8
(
15
)
and GPTQ
(
19
)
for reduced memory usage.

Table 53
:
Comprehensive Backend Comparison. Selection depends on deployment requirements, model type, and available hardware. vLLM provides best performance for local SLM deployment.

Backend

Best Use Case

Requirements

Key Features

Limitations

Cost

vLLM

Production SLMs, high throughput

GPU (CUDA), HuggingFace models

PagedAttention, continuous batching, tensor parallelism

CUDA only, limited model support

Free

Development, broad compatibility

CPU/GPU, any device

All HuggingFace models, quantization (int8/int4), Flash Attention

Slower inference

Free

OpenAI

API key, internet

State-of-art models, function calling, streaming

Cost per token, rate limits

Very Costly

Q.2.1
vLLM Backend

Optimized for local SLM deployment with high performance.

Key Technologies.

•

PagedAttention
: Virtual memory system for KV cache reducing memory fragmentation by 4
\times

•

Continuous Batching
: Dynamic batching of requests reducing latency by 2-3
\times

•

Tensor Parallelism
: Model sharding across multiple GPUs for large models

•

Optimized CUDA Kernels
: Custom kernels for attention, sampling, quantization

Configuration.

The vLLM backend configuration is shown below.

vLLM Backend Configuration

model:
name: Qwen/Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct
backend: vllm
gpu_memory_utilization: 0.9 # Fraction of GPU memory to use
tensor_parallel_size: 1 # Number of GPUs for tensor parallelism
max_model_len: 32768 # Maximum sequence length
enforce_eager: false # Use CUDA graph for faster inference
trust_remote_code: true # Allow custom model code

Q.2.2
Transformers Backend

Fallback backend with maximum compatibility and configuration options.

Advantages.

•

Universal Compatibility
: All HuggingFace models supported

•

Quantization
: Built-in int8 and int4 quantization via bitsandbytes

•

CPU Support
: Runs on CPU for development/testing

•

Flash Attention
: Automatic Flash Attention 2 when available

Q.2.3
API Backends

Unified API adapter layer supporting OpenAI, Anthropic, and Gemini.

OpenAI Backend.

The OpenAI model backend configuration is shown below.

OpenAI Configuration

model:
name: gpt-4-turbo-preview
api_key: ${OPENAI_API_KEY}
api_base: https://api.openai.com/v1 # Optional: custom endpoint
organization: ${OPENAI_ORG_ID} # Optional
generation_config:
max_tokens: 4096
temperature: 0.7
response_format: {type: "json_object"} # For structured outputs

Cost Tracking.

API backends automatically track token usage and estimated costs:

\text{Cost}(r)=\text{input\_tokens}(r)\cdot c_{\text{in}}+\text{output\_tokens}(r)\cdot c_{\text{out}}

(122)

where
c_{\text{in}}
and
c_{\text{out}}
are per-token costs (e.g., GPT-4: $0.01/1K input, $0.03/1K output).

Q.4
Model Loading Examples

Automatic Selection.

This section shows examples of automatic backend selections.

Automatic Backend Selection Examples

from effgen import Agent

# Automatic vLLM selection (if CUDA available)
agent1 = Agent(model="Qwen/Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct")

# Automatic OpenAI selection
agent2 = Agent(model="gpt-4", api_key="sk-...")

# Automatic Anthropic selection
agent3 = Agent(model="claude-3-opus-20240229", api_key="sk-ant-...")

Explicit Backend with Advanced Options.

This section shows advanced options provided with explicit backends.

Explicit Backend Configuration

from effgen import Agent

# Force Transformers with quantization
agent = Agent(
model="Qwen/Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct",
backend="transformers",
quantization="int8",
device_map="auto",
generation_config={
"max_tokens": 2048,
"temperature": 0.0,
"use_flash_attention": True
}
)

# vLLM with tensor parallelism (2 GPUs)
agent = Agent(
model="Qwen/Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct",
backend="vllm",
tensor_parallel_size=2,
gpu_memory_utilization=0.95
)

Q.5
Multi-Backend Deployment

For production systems requiring failover or load balancing:

Multi-Backend Failover Pattern

from effgen import Agent, ModelLoader

# Define fallback chain: vLLM -> Transformers -> OpenAI
loader = ModelLoader(
model="Qwen/Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct",
backends=["vllm", "transformers", "openai"],
fallback_on_error=True
)

agent = Agent(model_loader=loader)

# Automatically falls back if primary backend fails
response = agent.run("Complex query...")
print(f"Used backend: {response.metadata[’backend’]}")

Key Findings.

•

higher throughput than Transformers for local inference

•
